[2507.22472] Inference in a generalized Bradley-Terry model for paired comparisons with covariates and a growing number of subjects Inference in a generalized Bradley-Terry model for paired comparisons with covariates and a growing number of subjects Ting Yan Thanks: Department of Statistics, Central China Normal University, Wuhan, 430079, China. Email: tingyanty@mail.ccnu.edu.cn. Affiliation: Central China Normal University Abstract Motivated by the home-field advantage in sports, we propose a generalized Bradley–Terry model that incorporates covariate information for paired comparisons. It has an n n -dimensional merit parameter 𝜷 \boldsymbol{\beta} and a fixed-dimensional regression coefficient 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} for covariates. When the number of subjects n n approaches infinity and the number of comparisons between any two subjects is fixed, we show the uniform consistency of the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) ( 𝜷 ^ , 𝜸 ^ ) (\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}) of ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) (\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) Furthermore, we derive the asymptotic normal distribution of the MLE by characterizing its asymptotic representation. The asymptotic distribution of 𝜸 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} is biased, while that of 𝜷 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} is not. This phenomenon can be attributed to the different convergence rates of 𝜸 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} and 𝜷 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} . To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study to explore the asymptotic theory in paired comparison models with covariates in a high-dimensional setting. The consistency result is further extended to an Erdős–Rényi comparison graph with a diverging number of covariates. Numerical studies and a real data analysis demonstrate our theoretical findings. Key words : Asymptotic normality, Bradley-Terry model, Consistency, Covariate, Growing number of parameters. Mathematics Subject Classification: 60F05, 62J15, 62F12, 62E20. 1 Introduction When it was difficult for subjects to rank simultaneously based on the judgment of one person, they were arranged as follows: repeatedly compared to each other in pairs. Paired comparison data also arise in situations in which there are natural win-loss results between two subjects without the presence of a judge. Subjects could be teams, players, beverages, journals, and products. One of the fundamental problems in paired comparisons is the production of a ranking for all subjects. Because global rankings are not easily obtained for non round-robin tournaments, developing a statistical model to estimate rankings is desirable. The Bradley–Terry model ( Bradley and Terry 1952 ) is one of the most popular models for this purpose, dating back to at least 1929 ( Zermelo 1929 ) . This is occasionally referred to as the Bradley–Terry–Luce model ( Luce 1959 ) . It assigns one merit parameter β i \beta_{i} to each subject and postulates that subject i i beats subject j j with a probability e β i − β j / ( 1 + e β i − β j ) e^{\beta_{i}-\beta_{j}}/(1+e^{\beta_{i}-\beta_{j}}) , independent of other comparisons. The ranking of all the subjects is determined by their maximum likelihood estimator (MLE). Since the work of Bradley and Terry 1952 , it has had numerous applications, ranging from rankings of classical sports teams ( Masarotto and Varin 2012 ; Sire and Redner 2008 ; Whelan and Wodon 2020 ) and scientific journals ( Stigler 1994 ; Varin et al. 2016 ) to the quality of product brands ( Radlinski and Joachims 2007 ) , such as two brands of wine of some type, for multiclass classification ( Hastie and Tibshirani 1998 ; Clémençon and Vogel 2020 ) and crowdsourcing ( Chen et al. 2016 ) . Among many paired comparison models, the Bradley–Terry model is unique, satisfying the Bradley–Terry–Luce system ( Colonius 1980 ) . Hajek et al. 2014 and Shah et al. 2016 demonstrated that the MLE in the Bradley–Terry model is minimax-optimal for estimating the merit parameters in terms of the mean squared error. A detailed investigation of the Bradley–Terry model, including maximum likelihood estimation, hypothesis testing and goodness-of-fit tests of the model can be found in Section 4 of David 1988 . To facilitate a wide range of applications, some generalized models have been proposed ( Luce 1959 ; Rao and Kupper 1967 ; Davidson 1970 ; Huang et al. 2006 , e.g.) . Algorithms for solving MLEs in these models have been established ( Ford 1957 ; Hunter 2003 ; Vojnovic et al. 2019 , e.g.) . As highlighted by Agresti 2012 (p. 455), most sports have home-field advantage: A team is more likely to win when playing in its home city. He introduced a “home-field advantage” model by assuming the logit of the probability of home i i beating away j j is the merit difference β i − β j \beta_{i}-\beta_{j} plus an effect parameter γ \gamma , where γ \gamma indicates a home-field advantage if γ &gt; 0 \gamma&gt;0 . The home team of the two evenly matched teams has the probability exp ⁡ ( γ ) / ( 1 + exp ⁡ ( γ ) ) \exp(\gamma)/(1+\exp(\gamma)) of winning. We extend this model to a general form. Let Z i ​ j ​ k Z_{ijk} be a deterministic p p -dimensional vector denoting covariate information associated with the k k th comparison between subjects i i and j j , where the dimension p p is fixed. It is suitable to require Z i ​ j ​ k = − Z j ​ i ​ k Z_{ijk}=-Z_{jik} , because if something is advantageous to i i then it is disadvantageous to j j . We incorporate the covariate information into the Bradley–Terry model by specifying the winning probability of i i against j j as ℙ ⁡ ( i ​ wins ​ j | Z i ​ j ​ k , 𝜸 , β i , β j ) = exp ⁡ ( β i − β j + Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ​ 𝜸 ) 1 + exp ⁡ ( β i − β j + Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ​ 𝜸 ) , \mathbb{P}(i\mbox{~wins~}j|Z_{ijk},\boldsymbol{\gamma},\beta_{i},\beta_{j})=\frac{\exp(\beta_{i}-\beta_{j}+Z_{ijk}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma})}{1+\exp(\beta_{i}-\beta_{j}+Z_{ijk}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma})}, (1) where 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} is a p p -dimensional regression coefficient of the covariates and β i \beta_{i} is the merit parameter of i i . Under the restriction Z i ​ j ​ k = − Z j ​ i ​ k Z_{ijk}=-Z_{jik} , the probability above is well defined. We call it the covariate-Bradley–Terry model (abbreviated as “CBTM”) hereafter. The covariate Z i ​ j ​ k Z_{ijk} can be formalized according to the situations of the teams or the attributes of the subjects. If X i ​ k X_{ik} and X j ​ k X_{jk} denote p p -dimensional attributes of i i and j j in the k k th comparison, respectively, they can be used to construct vector Z i ​ j ​ k = 𝐠 ⁡ ( X i ​ k , X j ​ k ) Z_{ijk}=\mathbf{g}(X_{ik},X_{jk}) for an asymmetric vector function, where 𝐠 ⁡ ( 𝐱 , 𝐲 ) = − 𝐠 ⁡ ( 𝐲 , 𝐱 ) \mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=-\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}) . For instance, if we let 𝐠 ⁡ ( X i ​ k , X j ​ k ) \mathbf{g}(X_{ik},X_{jk}) be equal to X i ​ k − X j ​ k X_{ik}-X_{jk} , then we can measure the dissimilarity between the two subjects. As an example, if the game is played in the city of team i i or subject i i is listed first, then we let X i ​ k = 1 X_{ik}=1 and X j ​ k = 0 X_{jk}=0 ( p = 1 p=1 ), such that Z i ​ j ​ k = 1 Z_{ijk}=1 and Z j ​ i ​ k = − 1 Z_{jik}=-1 . In this case, the CBTM reduces to the home-field advantage model in Agresti 2012 . In several paired comparison situations, the number of subjects n n is typically large, whereas the number of comparisons m i ​ j m_{ij} for any pair ( i , j ) (i,j) is relatively small ( Simons and Yao 1999 , e.g.) . Specifically, all m i ​ j m_{ij} are bounded by a fixed constant (e.g., each pair of teams in the NBA plays at most four games in a regular season). Theoretical analysis of the Bradley–Terry model with a diverse number of subjects has received wide attention in recent years. We have elaborated on these after stating our main results. However, the existing high-dimensional paired comparison literature has little involvement in additional information (e.g., covariate), but for win-loss outcomes. As shown in Agresti 2012 , this can significantly influence the ranking. To determine how covariates influence the estimation of merit parameters in the high dimension, we drew Figure 1 to evaluate the ℓ ∞ \ell_{\infty} -error ‖ 𝜷 ^ − 𝜷 ‖ ∞ \|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{\infty} via a simulation study here, where 𝜷 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} was fitted using the maximum likelihood estimation in the original BTM (shorthand of Bradley–Terry model) and the CBTM with the correct model specification for comparison. The figure on the left shows that the error in the BTM increases rapidly with γ \gamma whereas the error in the CBTM changes only slightly. The former became increasingly larger than the latter for γ \gamma . From the right figure, we can see the following: the error in the BTM is larger than that in the CBTM when n n increases and γ \gamma is fixed. Even when n n increased to a sufficiently large value, the error in the BTM did not decrease. This indicates that the CBTM has a significant improvement over the BTM when there is covariate effect. This partly motivated the present study. Figure 1: The plots of the average values of ‖ 𝜷 ^ − 𝜷 ‖ ∞ \|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{\infty} changing with γ \gamma (when n n is fixed) in the left and changing with n n (when γ \gamma is fixed) in the right. The red color indicates the error with fitted values in the BTM without covariates while the black color indicates the error in the CBTM. All β i \beta_{i} s were independently generated from the uniform distribution U ⁡ ( 0 , 1 ) U(0,1) . Each pair had only one comparison. We set Z i ​ j ​ k = 1 Z_{ijk}=1 when i &lt; j i&lt;j and Z i ​ j ​ k = − 1 Z_{ijk}=-1 when i &gt; j i&gt;j . The win-loss outcomes were generated according to the CBTM. The average value of ‖ 𝜷 ^ − 𝜷 ‖ ∞ \|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{\infty} was recorded out of 100 100 repetitions. The contributions of this study are as follows. • When n n goes to infinity and all m i ​ j ( &gt; 0 ) m_{ij}(&gt;0) are fixed, we establish the upper bounds of ‖ 𝜷 ^ − 𝜷 ‖ ∞ \|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{\infty} and ‖ 𝜸 ^ − 𝜸 ‖ ∞ \|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{\infty} under mild conditions, where ( 𝜷 ^ , 𝜸 ^ ) (\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}) is the MLE of ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) (\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) . Roughly speaking, the former is in the order of O p ​ ( ( log ⁡ n / n ) 1 / 2 ) O_{p}((\log n/n)^{1/2}) while the latter is in the order of O p ​ ( log ⁡ n / n ) O_{p}(\log n/n) . This leads to the uniform consistency of the MLE. A key idea for the proof is that we use a two-stage method that alternatively obtains the ℓ ∞ \ell_{\infty} -error between an estimator 𝜷 ^ γ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\gamma} and 𝜷 \boldsymbol{\beta} for a given 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} and the ℓ ∞ \ell_{\infty} -error between an estimator 𝜸 ^ β \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\beta} and 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} for a given 𝜷 \boldsymbol{\beta} . • We derive the asymptotic normal distribution of the MLE by characterizing its asymptotic representation. This is proved by applying Taylor’s expansions to a series of functions constructed from likelihood equations and showing remainder terms in the expansions are asymptotically neglect. The asymptotic distribution of the MLE 𝜸 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} contains a bias term while there is no bias for 𝜷 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} . This is because of different convergence rates for 𝜷 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} and 𝜸 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} . • We further extend the consistency result to an Erdős-Rényi random graph with a diverging number of p n p_{n} , where the sampling probability is allowed to be close to the Erdős-Rényi lower bound ( Erdős and Rényi 1960 ) . Simulation studies and a real data analysis are conducted to illustrate the theoretical results. 1.1 Related work Studies on the Bradley–Terry model in high-dimensional settings have recently attracted significant interest. In a pioneering study, Simons and Yao 1999 proved the uniform consistency and asymptotic normal distribution of the MLE when the number of subjects approaches infinity and each pair has a fixed number of comparisons. To relax the dense comparison assumption, Yan et al. 2012 extended their results to a fixed sparse comparison graph by controlling the length from one subject to another subject. Han et al. 2020 further extended Simons and Yao 1999 ’s results to an Erdös–Rényi comparison graph under a weak sparsity condition on q n q_{n} , where q n q_{n} is the probability that any two subjects will be compared. Chen et al. 2019 established the ℓ ∞ \ell_{\infty} -error bounds for the spectral estimator and regularized the MLE, which leads to sample complexity for the top- K K rankings. Chen et al. 2021 further studied the ℓ ∞ \ell_{\infty} -error of the MLE and obtained the minimax rate for top- k k ranking. However, covariate information was not considered in these studies, which is the focus of this study. While revising this paper 1 1 1 An original version of was submitted to some journal on April 25, 2020. I make this manuscript public on ArXiv until now. , a new related work appears. Fan et al. 2024 extend the Bradley–Terry model to incorporate the covariate information, where the covariate term is ( X i − X j ) ⊤ ​ 𝜸 (X_{i}-X_{j})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma} and X i X_{i} denotes the individual-level attribute X i X_{i} of subject i i . In contrast, the covariate term in model ( 1 ) is Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ​ 𝜸 Z_{ijk}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma} , which contains the special case ( X i − X j ) ⊤ ​ 𝜸 (X_{i}-X_{j})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma} . It is clear that Fan et al. 2024 characterize only the individual level covariate information and do not address such covariates associated with each paired comparision (e.g., home-field advantage). In addition, our proof strategy is different from theirs, where Fan et al. 2024 analyze the consistency of the MLE by using a constrained maximum likelihood technique with a projected gradient descent algorithm and derive asymptotic distributions of the MLE by approximating the MLE via the minimizer of the quadratic approximation of the likelihood function. In this study, we use a two-stage technique that alternatively obtains the ℓ ∞ \ell_{\infty} -error between an estimator 𝜷 ^ γ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\gamma} and 𝜷 \boldsymbol{\beta} and the ℓ ∞ \ell_{\infty} -error between an estimator 𝜸 ^ β \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\beta} and 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} , to show the consistency of the MLE and characterize asymptotic representations to obtain its asymptotic distributions. Note that the CBTM can be recast into a logistic regression model. The “large N N , diverging p N p_{N} ” framework in generalized linear models (GLMs) has been explored, where N N is the sample size, and p N p_{N} is the dimension of the parameter space. Portnoy 1988 showed the asymptotic normality of the MLE in exponential family of distributions on independent and identically distributed samples when p N 2 = o ⁡ ( N ) p_{N}^{2}=o(N) . He and Shao 2000 built the asymptotic normality of M M -estimators when p N 2 ​ log ⁡ p N = o ⁡ ( N ) p_{N}^{2}\log p_{N}=o(N) . Wang 2011a established the consistency of the generalized estimating equations estimator when p N 2 = o ⁡ ( N ) p^{2}_{N}=o(N) and its asymptotic normality when p N 3 = o ⁡ ( N ) p^{3}_{N}=o(N) . In our asymptotic framework for the CBTM, p N 2 / N → 1 / 2 p_{N}^{2}/N\to 1/2 , not 0 0 , where p N = p + n p_{N}=p+n and N = n ⁡ ( n + 1 ) / 2 N=n(n+1)/2 if each pair has only one comparison. Therefore, these asymptotic results are not applicable in this case. A relevant work to GLMs is Liang and Du 2012 , who study the asymptotic regime p N = o ⁡ ( N ) p_{N}=o(N) in a logistic regression model. Let λ min ​ ( S N ) \lambda_{\min}(S_{N}) and λ max ​ ( S N ) \lambda_{\max}(S_{N}) denote the minimum and maximum eigenvalues of S N S_{N} , where S N = ∑ i = 1 N x i ​ x i ⊤ S_{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}x_{i}^{\top} and x i x_{i} is the p N p_{N} -dimensional covariate vector of individual i i . Assuming that c 1 ​ N ≤ λ min ​ ( S N ) ≤ λ max ​ ( S N ) ≤ c 2 ​ N c_{1}N\leq\lambda_{\min}(S_{N})\leq\lambda_{\max}(S_{N})\leq c_{2}N for two constants c 1 c_{1} and c 2 c_{2} , they show the asymptotical normality of the MLE by extending the proof strategy in Yin et al. 2006 for GLMs with fixed dimensions to an increasing dimension. In CBTM, the first n n diagonal entries of S N S_{N} are of the order of n n , because of the special structure of the design matrix for the merit parameters 𝜷 \boldsymbol{\beta} , whereas the last p p diagonal entries of S N S_{N} are on the order of n 2 n^{2} . Because of the different orders of the diagonal elements of S N S_{N} , the ratio λ max ​ ( S N ) / λ min ​ ( S N ) \lambda_{\max}(S_{N})/\lambda_{\min}(S_{N}) is not constant. In a broad simulation study, we found the following: λ max ​ ( S N ) / λ min ​ ( S N ) \lambda_{\max}(S_{N})/\lambda_{\min}(S_{N}) is of the order of O ⁡ ( N ) O(N) , far from the assumption that λ max ​ ( S N ) / λ min ​ ( S N ) ≤ c 2 / c 1 \lambda_{\max}(S_{N})/\lambda_{\min}(S_{N})\leq c_{2}/c_{1} , Therefore, the conditions in Liang and Du 2012 cannot be applied to CBTM. Interestingly, a recent study reported the following: Zhou et al. 2021 extended Yin et al. 2006 ’s proof to GLMs with a diverging number of covariates, which requires, except for the same conditions for S N S_{N} as in Liang and Du 2012 , the condition p N 2 / N → 0 p_{N}^{2}/N\to 0 to guarantee asymptotic normality rather than the weaker condition p N / N → 0 p_{N}/N\to 0 . In addition, the asymptotic distribution of the MLE in the aforementioned literature is not biased ( Haberman 1977 ; Portnoy 1988 ; Wang 2011a ; Liang and Du 2012 ; Zhou et al. 2021 , e.g.) . In sharp contrast to these studies, the asymptotic distribution of MLE 𝜸 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} has a bias term, whereas that of the MLE 𝜷 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} does not. This phenomenon is referred to as the incidental parameter problem in econometric literature ( Graham 2017 , e.g.,) which is caused by different convergence rates of 𝜸 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} and 𝜷 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} . In the network setting, the degree heterogeneity and the homophily have been modelled in a similar logistic regression form ( Graham 2017 ; Yan et al. 2019 , e.g.) . However, their focus are network features, which is different. In addition, the case with the increasing dimension of covariates is not studied in their works. Model ( 1 ) can also be represented as a log-linear model. Although the conditions for the existence of an MLE have been established ( Fienberg and Rinaldo 2012 ) , asymptotic theories remain lacking in high-dimensional cases ( Fienberg and Rinaldo 2007 ; Fienberg and Rinaldo 2012 , e.g.) . The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. In Section 2 , we present the maximum likelihood estimation. In Section 3 , we present the consistency and asymptotic normality of the MLE. In Section 4.1 , we extend the consistency result to an Erdős–Rényi comparison graph with a diverging number of covariates. In Section 5 , we perform a simulation and provide a real data analysis. We provide a summary and further discussion in section 6 . The proofs of these theorems are provided in Section 7 . The proofs of the supported lemmas and the proof of Theorem 4 are presented in the supplementary material A. Supplementary material B contains some additional result. 2 Maximum likelihood estimation Consider a set of n + 1 n+1 subjects labelled by “ 0 , … , n 0,\ldots,n ”. Let m i ​ j m_{ij} be the number of comparisons between i i and j j and a i ​ j ​ k a_{ijk} be the outcome in the k k th comparison, k = 1 , … , m i ​ j k=1,\ldots,m_{ij} , where a i ​ j ​ k a_{ijk} ( ∈ { 0 , 1 } \in\{0,1\} ) is an indictor variable denoting whether i i beats j j in the k k th comparison. That is, if i i wins j j , then a i ​ j ​ k = 1 a_{ijk}=1 ; otherwise, a i ​ j ​ k = 0 a_{ijk}=0 . We assume that m i ​ j ≤ m ∗ m_{ij}\leq m_{*} for all i ≠ j i\neq j and m ∗ m_{*} is a fixed constant. The win-loss results are recorded in a matrix: A = ( a i ​ j ) n × n A=(a_{ij})_{n\times n} , where a i ​ j a_{ij} is the number of i i beating j j and the diagonal elements a i ​ i a_{ii} are set to zero by default, i.e., a i ​ i = 0 a_{ii}=0 . Let d i = ∑ j ≠ i a i ​ j d_{i}=\sum_{j\neq i}a_{ij} be the total number of wins for subject i i and 𝜷 = ( β 1 , … , β n ) ⊤ \boldsymbol{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n})^{\top} . Because adding the same constant to all β i \beta_{i} results in the invariance of probability ( 1 ), we set β 0 = 0 \beta_{0}=0 for model identification, as in Simons and Yao 1999 . Other restrictions are possible; for example, ∑ i β i = 0 \sum_{i}\beta_{i}=0 . In model ( 1 ), the log-likelihood function is ℓ ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) = ∑ 0 ≤ i &lt; j ≤ n ∑ k = 1 m i ​ j { a i ​ j ​ k ​ ( β i − β j + Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ​ 𝜸 ) − log ⁡ ( 1 + e β i − β j + Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ​ 𝜸 ) } = ∑ i β i ​ d i + ∑ i &lt; j ∑ k a i ​ j ​ k ​ ( Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ​ 𝜸 ) − ∑ i &lt; j ∑ k log ⁡ ( 1 + exp ⁡ ( β i − β j + Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ​ 𝜸 ) ) . \begin{array}[]{rcl}\ell(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})&amp;=&amp;\sum\limits_{0\leq i&lt;j\leq n}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m_{ij}}\{a_{ijk}(\beta_{i}-\beta_{j}+Z_{ijk}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\log(1+e^{\beta_{i}-\beta_{j}+Z_{ijk}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\}\\ &amp;=&amp;\sum\limits_{i}\beta_{i}d_{i}+\sum\limits_{i&lt;j}\sum\limits_{k}a_{ijk}(Z_{ijk}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma})-\sum\limits_{i&lt;j}\sum\limits_{k}\log(1+\exp(\beta_{i}-\beta_{j}+Z_{ijk}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma})).\end{array} Write μ i ​ j ​ k ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) \mu_{ijk}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) as the expectation of a i ​ j ​ k a_{ijk} , where μ i ​ j ​ k ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) \mu_{ijk}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) is equal to the probability of i i winning j j in the k k th comparison given in ( 1 ). The maximum likelihood equation is as follows: d i = ∑ j = 0 , j ≠ i n ∑ k = 1 m i ​ j μ i ​ j ​ k ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) , i = 1 , … , n , ∑ 0 ≤ i &lt; j ≤ n ∑ k = 1 m i ​ j a i ​ j ​ k ​ Z i ​ j ​ k = ∑ 0 ≤ i &lt; j ≤ n ∑ k = 1 m i ​ j Z i ​ j ​ k ​ μ i ​ j ​ k ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) . \begin{array}[]{rcl}d_{i}&amp;=&amp;\sum\limits_{j=0,j\neq i}^{n}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m_{ij}}\mu_{ijk}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}),~~i=1,\ldots,n,\\ \sum\limits_{0\leq i&lt;j\leq n}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m_{ij}}a_{ijk}Z_{ijk}&amp;=&amp;\sum\limits_{0\leq i&lt;j\leq n}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{m_{ij}}Z_{ijk}\mu_{ijk}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}).\end{array} (2) It should be noted that the above equations do not contain d 0 d_{0} . This is because ∑ i = 0 n d i = ∑ i &lt; j m i ​ j \sum_{i=0}^{n}d_{i}=\sum_{i&lt;j}m_{ij} , The MLE of the parameter ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) (\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) , denoted as ( 𝜷 ^ , 𝜸 ^ ) (\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}) , is the solution to the above equations due to the convex of the log-likelihood function, where β ^ 0 = 0 \hat{\beta}_{0}=0 . Let 𝒦 \mathcal{K} be the convex hull of set { ( d 0 , … , d n , ∑ i &lt; j ∑ k Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ a i ​ j ​ k ) ⊤ : a i ​ j ​ k ∈ { 0 , 1 } , 0 ≤ i &lt; j ≤ n , k = 1 , … , m i ​ j } . \{(d_{0},\ldots,d_{n},\sum_{i&lt;j}\sum_{k}Z_{ijk}^{\top}a_{ijk})^{\top}:a_{ijk}\in\{0,1\},0\leq i&lt;j\leq n,k=1,\ldots,m_{ij}\}. As the normalizing function ∑ i &lt; j ∑ k log ⁡ ( 1 + exp ⁡ ( β i − β j + Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ​ 𝜸 ) ) \sum_{i&lt;j}\sum_{k}\log(1+\exp(\beta_{i}-\beta_{j}+Z_{ijk}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma})) is steep and strictly convex, by the properties of exponential family of distributions [e.g., Theorem 5.5 in Brown 1986 (p. 148)], we have the following result. Proposition 1 . The MLE ( 𝛃 ^ , 𝛄 ^ ) (\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}) exists if and only if ( d 0 , … , d n , ∑ i &lt; j ∑ k Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ​ a i ​ j ​ k ) ⊤ (d_{0},\ldots,d_{n},\sum_{i&lt;j}\sum_{k}Z_{ijk}^{\top}a_{ijk})^{\top} lies in the interior of 𝒦 \mathcal{K} . If the vector ( d 0 , … , d n ) (d_{0},\ldots,d_{n}) contains zero elements (corresponding to subjects without wins), or values being equal to the total number of comparisons of some subjects (corresponding to subjects without losses), this condition did not exist. If we do not consider covariate information, then this condition can be easily explained in terms of graph language. The win-loss matrix A A can be represented in a directed graph 𝒢 n \mathcal{G}_{n} with nodes denoting subjects and directed edges denote the number of wins for one subject against another. The necessary and sufficient condition for the existence of 𝜷 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} is that the directed graph 𝒢 n \mathcal{G}_{n} is strongly connected. That is, for every partition of subjects into two nonempty sets, a subject in the second set beats the subject in the first set at least once [ Ford 1957 ]. We discuss computational issues. For small n n , we can simply use the package “glm” in the R language to solve the MLE. For relatively large n n , it might not have large enough memory to store the design matrix for 𝜷 \boldsymbol{\beta} required by the “glm.” In this case, we recommend using a two-step iterative algorithm by alternating between solving the first equation in ( 2 ) using the fixed-point method in Ford 1957 and solving the second equation in ( 2 ) using the Newton-Raphson method. 3 Theoretical properties In this section, we present the consistency and asymptotic normality of the MLE. First, we introduce certain notation. For a subset C ⊂ ℝ n C\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} , let C 0 C^{0} and C ¯ \overline{C} denote the interior and closure of C C , respectively. For a vector 𝐱 = ( x 1 , … , x n ) ⊤ ∈ ℝ n \mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , we denote ‖ 𝐱 ‖ ∞ = max 1 ≤ i ≤ n ⁡ | x i | \|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|x_{i}| and ‖ 𝐱 ‖ 1 = ∑ i | x i | \|\mathbf{x}\|_{1}=\sum_{i}|x_{i}| by the ℓ ∞ \ell_{\infty} - and ℓ 1 \ell_{1} -norms of 𝐱 \mathbf{x} , respectively. Let B ⁡ ( 𝐱 , ϵ ) = { 𝐲 : ‖ 𝐱 − 𝐲 ‖ ∞ ≤ ϵ } B(\mathbf{x},\epsilon)=\{\mathbf{y}:\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon\} be the ϵ \epsilon -neighborhood of 𝐱 \mathbf{x} . For an n × n n\times n matrix J = ( J i ​ j ) J=(J_{ij}) , let ‖ J ‖ ∞ \|J\|_{\infty} denote the matrix norm induced by the ℓ ∞ \ell_{\infty} -norm on the vectors in ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} ; that is, ‖ J ‖ ∞ = max 𝐱 ≠ 0 ⁡ ‖ J ​ 𝐱 ‖ ∞ ‖ 𝐱 ‖ ∞ = max ⁡ ∑ j = 1 n 1 ≤ i ≤ n ⁡ | J i ​ j | , \|J\|_{\infty}=\max_{\mathbf{x}\neq 0}\frac{\|J\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}}{\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}}=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|J_{ij}|, where ‖ J ‖ \|J\| denotes a general matrix norm. Define the maximum absolute entry-wise norm: ‖ J ‖ max = max i , j ⁡ | J i ​ j | \|J\|_{\max}=\max_{i,j}|J_{ij}| . We use the superscript “*” to denote the true parameter under which the data are generated. When there is no ambiguity, we omit the superscript “*”. The notation ∑ i &lt; j \sum_{i&lt;j} is a shorthand for ∑ i = 0 n ∑ j = i + 1 n \sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j=i+1}^{n} . Define μ ⁡ ( x ) := e x 1 + e x , π i ​ j ​ k := β i − β j + Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ​ 𝜸 , π i ​ j ​ k ∗ := β i ∗ − β j ∗ + Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ​ 𝜸 ∗ . \mu(x):=\frac{e^{x}}{1+e^{x}},~~\pi_{ijk}:=\beta_{i}-\beta_{j}+Z_{ijk}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma},~~\pi_{ijk}^{*}:=\beta_{i}^{*}-\beta_{j}^{*}+Z_{ijk}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}. (3) The dependence of the expectation of a i ​ j ​ k a_{ijk} on these parameters is through π i ​ j ​ k \pi_{ijk} . We can also write μ ⁡ ( π i ​ j ​ k ) \mu(\pi_{ijk}) as the expectation of a i ​ j ​ k a_{ijk} . We will use the notations μ ⁡ ( π i ​ j ​ k ) \mu(\pi_{ijk}) and μ i ​ j ​ k ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) \mu_{ijk}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) interchangeably. c , c 0 , c 1 c,c_{0},c_{1} , C , C 0 , C 1 C,C_{0},C_{1} , … \ldots , refer to universal constants. The specific values may vary from place to place. We assume that all covariates Z i ​ j ​ k Z_{ijk} are bounded by a constant; that is, sup i , j , k ‖ Z i ​ j ​ k ‖ 2 ≤ c 1 \sup_{i,j,k}\|Z_{ijk}\|_{2}\leq c_{1} for a fixed constant c 1 c_{1} . In this section, we assume that the dimension of Z i ​ j ​ k Z_{ijk} is fixed. This condition is presented in Graham 2017 , Dzemski 2019 and Yan et al. 2019 . We do not consider unbounded covariates here, although our results can be extended to situations with a slow-increasing rate of z ∗ z_{*} . If Z i ​ j ​ k Z_{ijk} is not bounded, we can adopt the logistic transformation ( f ⁡ ( x ) = exp ⁡ ( x ) / ( 1 + exp ⁡ ( x ) CLOSE f(x)=\exp(x)/(1+\exp(x) ) to bound it. 3.1 Consistency To establish the consistency of the MLE, we introduce a system of score functions based on the maximum likelihood equations: H i ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) = ∑ j ≠ i ∑ k μ i ​ j ​ k ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) − d i , i = 0 , … , n , H ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) = ( H 1 ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) , … , H n ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) ) ⊤ . \begin{array}[]{rcl}H_{i}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})&amp;=&amp;\sum\nolimits_{j\neq i}\sum\nolimits_{k}\mu_{ijk}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})-d_{i},~~i=0,\ldots,n,\\ H(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})&amp;=&amp;(H_{1}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}),\ldots,H_{n}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}))^{\top}.\end{array} (4) Furthermore, we define H γ , i ​ ( 𝜷 ) H_{\gamma,i}(\boldsymbol{\beta}) as the value of H i ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) H_{i}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) for an arbitrarily fixed 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} , and H γ ​ ( 𝜷 ) = ( H γ , 1 ​ ( 𝜷 ) , … , H γ , n ​ ( 𝜷 ) ) ⊤ . H_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\beta})=(H_{\gamma,1}(\boldsymbol{\beta}),\ldots,H_{\gamma,n}(\boldsymbol{\beta}))^{\top}. Let 𝜷 ^ γ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\gamma} be the solution to H γ ​ ( 𝜷 ) = 0 H_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\beta})=0 . Correspondingly, we define two additional score functions: Q ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) \displaystyle Q(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) = \displaystyle= ∑ i &lt; j ∑ k Z i ​ j ​ k ​ { μ i ​ j ​ k ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) − a i ​ j ​ k } , \displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{i&lt;j}\sum\nolimits_{k}Z_{ijk}\{\mu_{ijk}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})-a_{ijk}\}, (5) Q c ​ ( 𝜸 ) \displaystyle Q_{c}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}) = \displaystyle= ∑ i &lt; j ∑ k Z i ​ j ​ k ​ { μ ⁡ ( β ^ γ , i − β ^ γ , j + Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ​ 𝜸 ) − a i ​ j ​ k } . \displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{i&lt;j}\sum\nolimits_{k}Z_{ijk}\{\mu(\widehat{\beta}_{\gamma,i}-\widehat{\beta}_{\gamma,j}+Z_{ijk}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma})-a_{ijk}\}. (6) Q c ​ ( 𝜸 ) Q_{c}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}) can be viewed as a concentrated or profiled function of Q ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) Q(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) , where the merit parameter 𝜷 \boldsymbol{\beta} was profiled. Clearly, if ( 𝜷 ^ , 𝜸 ^ ) (\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}) exist, then H ⁡ ( 𝜷 ^ , 𝜸 ^ ) = 0 , H ⁡ ( 𝜷 ^ γ , 𝜸 ) = H γ ​ ( 𝜷 ^ γ ) = 0 , Q ⁡ ( 𝜷 ^ , 𝜸 ^ ) = 0 , Q c ​ ( 𝜸 ^ ) = 0 . H(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})=0,~~H(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=H_{\gamma}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\gamma})=0,~~Q(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})=0,~~Q_{c}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})=0. Note that model ( 1 ) contains two sets of parameters: a merit vector parameter 𝜷 \boldsymbol{\beta} with a growing dimension, and regression coefficient 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} of covariates with fixed dimensions. If we employ the classical strategy for the proof of consistency that aims to show the log-likelihood function ℓ ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) \ell(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) has its maximum value in an ϵ \epsilon -neighborhood around the true parameter, we face two significant challenges: addressing an increasing dimension problem and addressing the non-identical distribution across observations. It is unclear which techniques can be used to address them. In the absence of covariates, Simons and Yao 1999 proved the consistency of the MLE through two key steps that first bound the probability that the strong connection condition in the win-loss comparison graphs failed. Then, we find a set of common neighborhoods with ratios close to the maximum ratio u ^ i 0 / u i 0 \hat{u}_{i_{0}}/u_{i_{0}} and the minimum ratio u ^ i 1 / u i 1 \hat{u}_{i_{1}}/u_{i_{1}} , where u ^ i = e β ^ i \hat{u}_{i}=e^{\hat{\beta}_{i}} and u i = e β i u_{i}=e^{\beta_{i}} , i 0 = arg ⁡ max i ⁡ u ^ i / u i i_{0}=\arg\max_{i}\hat{u}_{i}/u_{i} and i 1 = arg ⁡ min i ⁡ u ^ i / u i i_{1}=\arg\min_{i}\hat{u}_{i}/u_{i} . The first step establishes the existence of an MLE with a high probability. In the presence of covariates, it is difficult to verify the existence of the MLE. In addition, it is unclear how to find such neighborhoods because the appearance of covariates will make some key inequalities in Simons and Yao 1999 be difficult to generalize. We exploit the convergence rate of the Newton iterative algorithm to solve the equation F ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) = 0 F(\mathbf{x})=0 for showing consistency. Under the well-known Newton-Kantorovich conditions [ Kantorovich 1948 ], the algorithm converges and exhibits a high geometric convergence rate. As a result, a solution to the equation exists, and an ℓ p \ell_{p} -error between the initial and limiting points is obtained. Because the dimension increases with n n it is difficult to obtain in a single step for the full parameter vector ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) (\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) . To overcome this limitation, we use a two-stage process that alternatively obtains the upper bound of the error between 𝜷 ^ γ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\gamma} and 𝜷 ∗ \boldsymbol{\beta}^{*} with a given 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} , and derives the upper bound of the error between 𝜸 ^ β \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\beta} and 𝜸 ∗ \boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*} with a given 𝜷 \boldsymbol{\beta} . From the likelihood perspective, the two-stage process corresponds to maximizing ℓ ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) \ell(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) in two steps: First, we maximize ℓ ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) \ell(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) with respect to 𝜷 \boldsymbol{\beta} for fixed 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} . then insert the maximizing value of 𝜷 \boldsymbol{\beta} back into ℓ \ell and maximize ℓ \ell with respect to 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} . We need a condition on the design matrix for the regression coefficient 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} of the covariates. Condition 1 . There exists a constant c 0 c_{0} such that λ min ​ ( ∑ i &lt; j ∑ k Z i ​ j ​ k ​ Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ) ≥ c 0 ​ n 2 , \lambda_{\min}(\sum_{i&lt;j}\sum_{k}Z_{ijk}Z_{ijk}^{\top})\geq c_{0}n^{2}, (7) where λ min ​ ( A ) \lambda_{\min}(A) denotes the minimum eigenvalue of a general matrix A A . The above condition is widely used in high-dimensional GLMs ( Haberman 1977 ; Portnoy 1988 ; Wang 2011a ; Liang and Du 2012 ; Zhou et al. 2021 ) . If Z i ​ j ​ k Z_{ijk} are independently generated from some non-degenerate multivariate distribution, then the condition holds. Condition 2 . The true vector parameters 𝛃 ∗ \boldsymbol{\beta}^{*} and 𝛄 ∗ \boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*} lie in a compact set. Condition 2 implies that ‖ 𝜷 ∗ ‖ ∞ \|\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\|_{\infty} and ‖ 𝜸 ∗ ‖ ∞ \|\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}\|_{\infty} are bounded above by a positive constant. In high dimensional GLMs, it is generally assumed that the model parameter is bounded above by a constant in terms of ℓ 2 \ell_{2} -norm ( Wang 2011a , e.g.,) . In addition, ‖ 𝜷 ∗ ‖ ∞ ≤ c \|\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq c is made in Chen et al. 2020 . We now formally state the consistency. Theorem 1 . If Conditions 1 and 2 hold, then, with a probability of at least 1 − O ⁡ ( n − 1 ) 1-O(n^{-1}) , the MLE ( 𝛃 ^ , 𝛄 ^ ) (\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}) exists, and satisfies ‖ 𝜷 ^ − 𝜷 ∗ ‖ ∞ = O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n n ) , ‖ 𝜸 ^ − 𝜸 ∗ ‖ 2 = O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n n ) . \|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\|_{\infty}=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}\right),\quad\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}\|_{2}=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}\right). (8) Remark 1 . We compared our ℓ ∞ \ell_{\infty} -error bound with Simons and Yao 1999 in the case of no covariates. They show that ‖ 𝛃 ^ − 𝛃 ‖ ∞ = O p ​ ( ( log ⁡ n / n ) 1 / 2 ) \|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}((\log n/n)^{1/2}) when ‖ β ∗ ‖ ∞ \|\beta^{*}\|_{\infty} is bounded by a constant, our result is consistent with the minimax error bound in Simons and Yao 1999 and Chen et al. 2020 , up to some constant factor. Remark 2 . The error bound for 𝛃 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} match the minimax optimal bound ‖ 𝛃 ^ − 𝛃 ‖ ∞ = O p ​ ( ( log ⁡ p N / N ) 1 / 2 ) \|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}((\log p_{N}/N)^{1/2}) for the LASSO estimator in a linear model with p N p_{N} -dimensional parameter β \beta and sample size N N in Lounici 2008 . In our case, there are N = n ⁡ ( n − 1 ) / 2 N=n(n-1)/2 observed edges and a p N = ( p + n ) p_{N}=(p+n) -dimensional parameter space. However, the error bound for 𝛄 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} is much slower than the optimal convergence rate N − 1 / 2 N^{-1/2} in classical large-sample theory. The asymptotic distribution result in Theorem 2 shows that the convergence rate of 𝛄 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} is in the order of O p ​ ( 1 / n ) O_{p}(1/n) being optimal. We apply the consistency result to the top- K K recovery problem, which identifies a set of K K subjects with the highest ranks. This problem has received considerable attention in machine learning research; see Chen et al. 2019 and references therein. We assume that there is a ground-truth order β 0 ∗ &gt; β 1 ∗ &gt; ⋯ &gt; β n ∗ \beta_{0}^{*}&gt;\beta_{1}^{*}&gt;\cdots&gt;\beta_{n}^{*} . The aim is to find subjects with K K largest estimates in accordance with their true orders. It suffices to demonstrate that β ^ i − β ^ j &gt; 0 , i = 0 , … , K − 1 ; j = K , … , n . \widehat{\beta}_{i}-\widehat{\beta}_{j}&gt;0,~~i=0,\ldots,K-1;j=K,\ldots,n. As in Chen et al. 2019 , we require a separation measure Δ K = β K − 1 ∗ − β K ∗ \Delta_{K}=\beta^{*}_{K-1}-\beta^{*}_{K} to distinguish between the ( K − 1 ) (K-1) th and K K th subjects. From the triangle inequality, we obtain: β ^ i − β ^ j ≥ β i ∗ − β j ∗ − | β ^ i − β i ∗ | − | β ^ j − β j ∗ | ≥ Δ K − O p ​ ( log ⁡ n n ) . \widehat{\beta}_{i}-\widehat{\beta}_{j}\geq\beta_{i}^{*}-\beta_{j}^{*}-|\widehat{\beta}_{i}-\beta_{i}^{*}|-|\widehat{\beta}_{j}-\beta_{j}^{*}|\geq\Delta_{K}-O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}\right). Therefore, we have the following corollary: Corollary 1 . We assume that the condition in Theorem 1 holds. If Δ K ≫ ( log ⁡ n n ) 1 / 2 \Delta_{K}\gg(\frac{\log n}{n})^{1/2} , with a probability of at least 1 − O ⁡ ( n − 1 ) 1-O(n^{-1}) , the set of top- K K -ranked items can be recovered exactly by using MLE. 3.2 Asymptotic normality of γ ^ \widehat{\gamma} Let ℓ c ​ ( 𝜸 ) \ell_{c}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}) be the concentrated log-likelihood function of ℓ ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) \ell(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) by replacing 𝜷 \boldsymbol{\beta} with 𝜷 ^ γ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\gamma} . It is easy to verify that the Hessian matrix of − ℓ c ​ ( 𝜸 ) -\ell_{c}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}) (i.e., the Jacobian matrix Q c ′ ​ ( 𝜸 ) Q_{c}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}) ) is Σ ⁡ ( 𝜷 ^ , 𝜸 ) \Sigma(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) , where Σ ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) := ∂ Q ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) ∂ 𝜸 ⊤ − ∂ Q ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) ∂ 𝜷 ⊤ ​ [ ∂ H ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) ∂ 𝜷 ⊤ ] − 1 ​ ∂ H ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) ∂ 𝜸 ⊤ . \Sigma(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}):=\frac{\partial Q(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})}{\partial\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\top}}-\frac{\partial Q(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\top}}\left[\frac{\partial H(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\top}}\right]^{-1}\frac{\partial H(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})}{\partial\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\top}}. (9) Note that Q c ′ ​ ( 𝜸 ) Q_{c}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}) is the Fisher information on 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} , which measures the amount of information on 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} provided by win-loss outcomes. Therefore, the asymptotic distribution of 𝜸 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} depends crucially on Q c ′ ​ ( 𝜸 ) Q_{c}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}) . Note that ( 9 ) involves with the inverse of ∂ H ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) / ∂ 𝜷 ⊤ \partial H(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})/\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\top} , which is denoted as H γ ′ ​ ( 𝜷 ) H^{\prime}_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\beta}) for convenience. In general, the inverse of H γ ′ ​ ( 𝜷 ) H^{\prime}_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\beta}) does not have a closed form. We use a simple matrix to approximate it. The Jacobian matrix H γ ′ ​ ( 𝜷 ) H^{\prime}_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\beta}) has a special structure that can be characterized in the form of a matrix class. Given b 0 , b 1 &gt; 0 b_{0},b_{1}&gt;0 , we say that an n × n n\times n -matrix V = ( v i ​ j ) i , j = 1 n V=(v_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n} belongs to the matrix class ℒ n ​ ( b 0 , b 1 ) \mathcal{L}_{n}(b_{0},b_{1}) if V V is a diagonally dominant matrix with negative nondiagonal elements bounded by b 0 b_{0} and b 1 b_{1} ; that is, b 0 ≤ v i ​ i + ∑ j = 1 , j ≠ i n v i ​ j ≤ b 1 , i = 1 , … , n , b 0 ≤ − v i ​ j ≤ b 1 , i , j = 1 , … , n ; i ≠ j . \begin{array}[]{l}b_{0}\leq v_{ii}+\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{n}v_{ij}\leq b_{1},~~i=1,\ldots,n,\\ b_{0}\leq-v_{ij}\leq b_{1},~~i,j=1,\ldots,n;i\neq j.\end{array} Define v 0 ​ n = v n ​ 0 = ∑ j = 1 , j ≠ i n v i ​ j − v i ​ i v_{0n}=v_{n0}=\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{n}v_{ij}-v_{ii} for i = 1 , … , n i=1,\ldots,n and v 00 = − ∑ i = 1 n v i ​ n v_{00}=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{in} . Simons and Yao 1998 proposed to approximate the inverse of V V , V − 1 V^{-1} , by a simple matrix S = ( s i ​ j ) n × n S=(s_{ij})_{n\times n} , where s i ​ j = δ i ​ j v i ​ i + 1 v 00 . s_{ij}=\frac{\delta_{ij}}{v_{ii}}+\frac{1}{v_{00}}. (10) In the above equation, δ i ​ j = 1 \delta_{ij}=1 if i = j i=j ; otherwise, δ i ​ j = 0 \delta_{ij}=0 . It is clear that H γ ′ ​ ( 𝜷 ) H^{\prime}_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\beta}) belongs to this matrix class. Hereafter, we denote V = ( v i ​ j ) V=(v_{ij}) by H γ ∗ ′ ​ ( 𝜷 ∗ ) H^{\prime}_{\gamma^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}) . Let N = ( n + 1 ) ​ n / 2 N=(n+1)n/2 and Σ ¯ := lim n → ∞ 1 N ​ Σ ​ ( 𝜷 ∗ , 𝜸 ∗ ) , \bar{\Sigma}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\Sigma(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}), where Σ ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) \Sigma(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) is defined in ( 9 ). We assume that the limit Σ ¯ \bar{\Sigma} exists, which was considered in Graham 2017 . By using S S in ( 10 ) to approximate V − 1 V^{-1} , we have 1 N ​ Σ ​ ( 𝜷 ∗ , 𝜸 ∗ ) = 1 N ​ ∑ i &lt; j ∑ k Z i ​ j ​ k ​ Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ ​ μ ′ ​ ( π i ​ j ​ k ∗ ) − 1 N ​ ∑ i Z ~ i ​ Z ~ i ⊤ v i ​ i + o ⁡ ( 1 ) , \displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\Sigma(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*})=\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i&lt;j}\sum\limits_{k}Z_{ijk}Z_{ijk}^{\top}\mu^{\prime}(\pi_{ijk}^{*})-\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i}\frac{\tilde{Z}_{i}\tilde{Z}_{i}^{\top}}{v_{ii}}+o(1), (11) where Z ~ i = ∑ j ≠ i ∑ k Z i ​ j ​ k ​ μ ′ ​ ( π i ​ j ​ k ∗ ) . \tilde{Z}_{i}=\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{k}Z_{ijk}\mu^{\prime}(\pi_{ijk}^{*}). If Z i ​ j ​ k Z_{ijk} is independently draw from some multivariate distribution, then N − 1 ​ Σ ​ ( 𝜷 ∗ , 𝜸 ∗ ) N^{-1}\Sigma(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}) converges in probability to some non-random matrix. The idea of establishing the asymptotic normality of 𝜸 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} is briefly described as follows: First, we use the mean-value expansion to derive the explicit expression of 𝜸 ^ − 𝜸 ∗ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*} , which contains term Q c ​ ( 𝜸 ∗ ) Q_{c}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}) multiplied by Σ ¯ − 1 \bar{\Sigma}^{-1} . Then, we apply a third-order Taylor expansion to Q c ​ ( 𝜸 ∗ ) Q_{c}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}) to characterize its limiting distribution. In the expansion, the first-order term is asymptotically normal; the second-order term is the asymptotic bias term and the first-order term is the remainder term. The asymptotic normality of 𝜸 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} is described as follows. Theorem 2 . Suppose that the conditions in Theorem 1 hold. For a nonzero constant vector 𝐜 = ( c 1 , … , c p ) ⊤ \mathbf{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{p})^{\top} , N ​ 𝐜 ⊤ ​ ( 𝛄 ^ − 𝛄 ) \sqrt{N}\mathbf{c}^{\top}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}) converges in distribution to normal distribution with mean Σ ¯ − 1 ​ B ∗ \bar{\Sigma}^{-1}B_{*} and variance c ⊤ ​ Σ ¯ ​ 𝐜 c^{\top}\bar{\Sigma}\mathbf{c} , B ∗ = lim n → ∞ 1 2 ​ N ​ ∑ i = 0 n ∑ j ≠ i ∑ k Z i ​ j ​ k ​ μ ′′ ​ ( π i ​ j ​ k ∗ ) ∑ j ≠ i ∑ k μ ′ ​ ( π i ​ j ​ k ∗ ) . B_{*}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=0}^{n}\frac{\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{k}Z_{ijk}\mu^{\prime\prime}(\pi^{*}_{ijk})}{\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{k}\mu^{\prime}(\pi^{*}_{ijk})}. (12) Remark 3 . The bias term is bounded above by a constant. This is due to that μ ′ ​ ( π i ​ j ​ k ∗ ) ≥ c 1 \mu^{\prime}(\pi^{*}_{ijk})\geq c_{1} and | μ ′′ ​ ( π i ​ j ​ k ∗ ) | ≤ c 2 |\mu^{\prime\prime}(\pi^{*}_{ijk})|\leq c_{2} for some constants c 1 c_{1} and c 2 c_{2} under the conditions in Theorem 1 . If λ min ​ ( Σ ⁡ ( 𝛃 ∗ , 𝛄 ∗ ) ) ≥ c 0 ​ n 2 \lambda_{\min}(\Sigma(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}))\geq c_{0}n^{2} , then 𝛄 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} has a convergence rate O ⁡ ( n − 1 ) O(n^{-1}) . If all Z i ​ j ​ k Z_{ijk} are centered and independently generated from subeponential distributions (or bounded random vectors), then ∑ j ≠ i ∑ k Z i ​ j ​ k ​ μ ′′ ​ ( π i ​ j ​ k ∗ ) \sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{k}Z_{ijk}\mu^{\prime\prime}(\pi^{*}_{ijk}) is of the order ( n ​ log ⁡ n ) 1 / 2 (n\log n)^{1/2} with probability 1 − O ⁡ ( n − 1 ) 1-O(n^{-1}) . This can be easily verified by the concentration inequality for sub-exponential random variables or by Hoeffding 1963 ’s inequality for bounded random variables. In this case, ‖ B ∗ ‖ ∞ = o p ​ ( 1 ) \|B_{*}\|_{\infty}=o_{p}(1) . For example, if all teams are played at home or at away equally likely, B ∗ B_{*} is asymptotically neglected, as demonstrated in our simulations. In other cases, the bias B ∗ B_{*} cannot be neglected. If so, we can use the analytical bias-correction formula as in Dzemski 2019 : 𝛄 ^ b ​ c = 𝛄 ^ − N − 1 / 2 Σ ^ − 1 ( 𝛃 ^ , 𝛄 ^ ) B ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{bc}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-N^{-1/2}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-1}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\hat{B} , where B ^ \widehat{B} and Σ ^ \widehat{\Sigma} are the estimates of B ∗ B_{*} and Σ ¯ \bar{\Sigma} obtained by replacing 𝛃 ∗ \boldsymbol{\beta}^{*} and 𝛄 ∗ \boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*} in their expressions with the estimators 𝛃 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} and 𝛄 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} . Remark 4 . The asymptotic distribution of 𝛄 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} contains a bias term B ∗ B_{*} . This is because of the different convergence rates of 𝛄 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} and 𝛃 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} , which roughly are O p ​ ( 1 / n ) O_{p}(1/n) and O p ​ ( 1 / n 1 / 2 ) O_{p}(1/n^{1/2}) . This phenomenon is referred to as the incidental parameter problem; see econometric literature Graham 2017 and the references therein. 3.3 Asymptotic normality of β ^ \widehat{\beta} The idea of establishing an asymptotic distribution 𝜷 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} is briefly described as follows. A second-order Taylor expansion is applied to H γ ​ ( 𝜷 ^ ) H_{\gamma}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}) at 𝜷 \boldsymbol{\beta} to derive the following explicit asymptotic expression for 𝜷 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} . In the expansion, the first-order term is the sum of [ H γ ′ ​ ( 𝜷 ^ ) ] − 1 ​ ( 𝜷 ^ − 𝜷 ) [H^{\prime}_{\gamma}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})]^{-1}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}) and V γ ​ β ​ ( 𝜸 ^ − 𝜸 ) V_{\gamma\beta}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}) , where V γ ​ β = ∂ H ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) / ∂ 𝜸 ⊤ V_{\gamma\beta}=\partial H(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})/\partial\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\top} . Because [ H γ ′ ​ ( 𝜷 ) ] − 1 [H^{\prime}_{\gamma}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})]^{-1} does not have a closed form, we use S S defined in ( 10 ) to approximate it. From Theorem 2 , 𝜸 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} has an n − 1 n^{-1} convergence rate up to a factor. This makes that the term V γ ​ β ​ ( 𝜸 ^ − 𝜸 ) V_{\gamma\beta}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}) is an asymptotically neglected remainder term. The second-order term in the expansion is also asymptotically neglected. Then, we represent 𝜷 ^ − 𝜷 \widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta} as the sum of S ⁡ ( 𝐝 − 𝔼 ​ 𝐝 ) S(\mathbf{d}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{d}) and remaining terms, where 𝐝 = ( d 1 , … , d n ) ⊤ \mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})^{\top} . Therefore, the central limit theorem is proven by establishing the asymptotic normality of S ⁡ ( 𝐝 − 𝔼 ​ 𝐝 ) S(\mathbf{d}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{d}) and indicating that the remaining terms are negligible. We formally state the central limit theorem as follows: Theorem 3 . Assume that λ min ​ ( Σ ⁡ ( 𝛃 ∗ , 𝛄 ∗ ) ) ≥ c 0 ​ n 2 \lambda_{\min}(\Sigma(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}))\geq c_{0}n^{2} . If Conditions 1 and 2 hold, then, for a fixed k k the vectors ( ( β ^ 1 − β 1 ∗ ) , … , ( β ^ k − β k ∗ ) ) ((\widehat{\beta}_{1}-\beta^{*}_{1}),\ldots,(\widehat{\beta}_{k}-\beta^{*}_{k})) follows a k k -dimensional multivariate normal distribution with a covariance matrix given by the upper left k × k k\times k block of S S defined in ( 10 ). Remark 5 . As discussed in Remark 3 , λ min ​ ( Σ ⁡ ( 𝛃 ∗ , 𝛄 ∗ ) ) ≥ c 0 ​ n 2 \lambda_{\min}(\Sigma(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}))\geq c_{0}n^{2} guarantees that 𝛄 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} has a convergence rate of O p ​ ( 1 / n ) O_{p}(1/n) . It leads to the remainder terms involved with 𝛄 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}} vanish. The asymptotic variance of β i ^ \widehat{\beta_{i}} is 1 / v i ​ i + 1 / v 00 1/v_{ii}+1/v_{00} , which is in the magnitudes of O ⁡ ( n 1 / 2 ) O(n^{1/2}) . In case of no covariates, it is consistent with that in Simons and Yao 1999 . 4 Extensions 4.1 Extension to an Erdős–Rényi comparison graph with a diverging number of covariates All the preceding results concern dense comparisons, where each pair has at least one comparison. We extended these to an Erdős–Rényi comparison graph 𝒢 ⁡ ( n , q n ) \mathcal{G}(n,q_{n}) , where any two subjects are compared with probability q n q_{n} . If q n → 0 q_{n}\to 0 , this implies a sparse comparison design. We assume that if two subjects are compared, they are compared at most m ∗ m_{*} times with m ∗ m_{*} fixed, according to the aforementioned settings. In addition, we consider the case of an increasing dimension of covariates, i.e., p n → ∞ p_{n}\to\infty . When p p depends on n n , we write p n p_{n} instead of p p . The consistency result is stated below, whose proof is in the supplementary material A. Theorem 4 . Assume that ‖ 𝛃 ∗ ‖ ∞ \|\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\|_{\infty} and ‖ 𝛄 ∗ ‖ 2 \|\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}\|_{2} are bounded by a positive constant, and q n ≥ c 1 ​ log ⁡ n / n q_{n}\geq c_{1}\log n/n for a sufficiently large constant c 1 c_{1} . If condition 1 holds, p n 2 = o ⁡ ( n ​ q n / log ⁡ n ) p_{n}^{2}=o(nq_{n}/\log n) and κ = sup i , j , k ‖ Z i ​ j ​ k ‖ 2 ≤ C \kappa=\sup_{i,j,k}\|Z_{ijk}\|_{2}\leq C for some constant C C , then, with a probability of at least 1 − O ⁡ ( n − 1 ) 1-O(n^{-1}) , the MLE ( 𝛃 ^ , 𝛄 ^ ) (\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}) exists and satisfies ‖ 𝜷 ^ − 𝜷 ∗ ‖ ∞ = O ⁡ ( log ⁡ n n ​ q n ) , ‖ 𝜸 ^ − 𝜸 ∗ ‖ 2 = O ⁡ ( p n ​ log ⁡ n n ​ q n ) . \|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\|_{\infty}=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nq_{n}}}\right),~~\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}\|_{2}=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{p_{n}\log n}{nq_{n}}}\right). When sampling probability q n q_{n} is less than log ⁡ n / n \log n/n , the realized comparison graph is disconnected with a positive probability according to the theory of the Erdős–Rényi graph. In this case, all subjects can be divided into two groups such that any subject in the first group does not have comparisons with any subject in the second group, where it is not possible to give a ranking of all subjects. Therefore, q n q_{n} should be not smaller than log ⁡ n / n \log n/n , up to a constant factor, which is a fundamental requirement to guarantee the connection of the sampling graph. Condition p n 2 = o ⁡ ( n ​ q n / log ⁡ n ) p_{n}^{2}=o(nq_{n}/\log n) restricts the increasing rate of p n p_{n} , which reduces to the condition in Wang 2011a when q n q_{n} is a constant. 4.2 Extensions to a fixed sparse comparison graph with a dynamic range of merit parameters We extend them to a fixed sparse comparison graph in Yan et al. 2012 here. In some applications such as sports, the comparison graph may be fixed, not be random. For example, in the regular season of the National Football League (NFL), which teams having games are scheduled in advance. More specially, there are 32 32 teams in the two conferences of the NFL and are divided into eight divisions each consisting of four teams. In the regular season, each team plays 16 16 matches, 6 6 within the division and 10 10 between the divisions. Motivated by the design of the regular season of the National Football League, they proposed a sparse condition to control the length from one subject to another subject with 2 2 or 3 3 : τ n := min 0 ≤ i &lt; j ≤ n # { k : m i ​ k &gt; 0 , m j ​ k &gt; 0 } n . \tau_{n}:=\min_{0\leq i&lt;j\leq n}\frac{\#\{k:m_{ik}&gt;0,m_{jk}&gt;0\}}{n}. That is, τ n \tau_{n} is the minimum ratio of the total number of paths between any i i and j j with length 2 2 or 3 3 . We assume that if two subjects have comparisons, they are compared at most m ∗ m_{*} times with m ∗ m_{*} fixed, in accordance with the aforementioned setting. The same proof technique can be readily extended to the setting here. The main different places are the error bound of using S S to approximate V − 1 V^{-1} , ‖ V − 1 − S ‖ max \|V^{-1}-S\|_{\max} , and the number of comparisons of subject i i , m i m_{i} , that will be replaced with b n 3 / ( n 2 ​ τ n 3 ) b_{n}^{3}/(n^{2}\tau_{n}^{3}) and n ​ τ n n\tau_{n} in the sparse case. Here, V = H γ ∗ ′ ​ ( 𝜷 ∗ ) V=H^{\prime}_{\gamma^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}) . Define b n := max i , j , k ⁡ ( 1 + e π i ​ j ​ k ∗ ) 2 e π i ​ j ​ k ∗ = = O ⁡ ( e max i , j ⁡ ( β i ∗ − β j ∗ ) + z ∗ ​ ‖ 𝜸 ∗ ‖ 1 ) , b_{n}:=\max_{i,j,k}\frac{(1+e^{\pi_{ijk}^{*}})^{2}}{e^{\pi_{ijk}^{*}}}==O(e^{\max_{i,j}(\beta_{i}^{*}-\beta_{j}^{*})+z_{*}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}\|_{1}}), (13) where z ∗ = max i , j , k ⁡ ‖ Z i ​ j ​ k ‖ ∞ z_{*}=\max_{i,j,k}\|Z_{ijk}\|_{\infty} . That says min i , j , k ⁡ μ ′ ​ ( π i ​ j ​ k ∗ ) ≥ 1 / b n \min_{i,j,k}\mu^{\prime}(\pi_{ijk}^{*})\geq 1/b_{n} . It is easy to see b n ≥ 4 b_{n}\geq 4 . Let λ min ​ ( 𝜷 ) \lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\beta}) be the smallest eigenvalue of n − 2 ​ Σ ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ∗ ) n^{-2}\Sigma(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}) and define ρ n := sup 𝜷 ∈ B ⁡ ( 𝜷 ∗ , ϵ n ​ 1 ) 2 λ min ​ ( 𝜷 ) . \rho_{n}:=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in B(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\epsilon_{n1})}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\beta})}. (14) Let ‖ A ‖ 2 \|A\|_{2} be the ℓ 2 \ell_{2} -norm of a matrix A A induced by Euclidean norm on vectors. By the inequality of matrix norm, as in Golub and Van Loan 1996 (p. 56–57), we have sup 𝜷 ∈ Σ ⁡ ( 𝜷 ∗ , ϵ n ​ 1 ) ‖ Σ − 1 ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ∗ ) ‖ ∞ ≤ sup 𝜷 ∈ Σ ⁡ ( 𝜷 ∗ , ϵ n ​ 1 ) 2 ​ ‖ Σ − 1 ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ∗ ) ‖ 2 ≤ ρ n n 2 . \sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\Sigma(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\epsilon_{n1})}\|\Sigma^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*})\|_{\infty}\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\Sigma(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\epsilon_{n1})}\sqrt{2}\|\Sigma^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*})\|_{2}\leq\frac{\rho_{n}}{n^{2}}. (15) Note that the dimension of the matrix Σ ⁡ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) \Sigma(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) is fixed and every its entry is a sum of n ⁡ ( n − 1 ) / 2 n(n-1)/2 terms. There it is suitable to have a factor n − 2 n^{-2} in the above inequality. We have the following theorem, whose proof is in the supplementary material B. Theorem 5 . (1) If ρ n ​ b n 9 / τ n 9 = o ⁡ ( ( n / log ⁡ n ) 1 / 2 ) \rho_{n}b_{n}^{9}/\tau_{n}^{9}=o((n/\log n)^{1/2}) , then with probability at least 1 − O ⁡ ( n − 1 ) 1-O(n^{-1}) , the MLE ( 𝛃 ^ , 𝛄 ^ ) (\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}) exists and satisfies OPEN ‖ 𝜸 ^ − 𝜸 ∗ ‖ ∞ = O p ​ ( ρ n ​ b n 9 ​ log ⁡ n n ​ τ n 9 ) ) = o p ​ ( 1 ) , ‖ 𝜷 ^ − 𝜷 ∗ ‖ ∞ = O p ​ ( b n 3 τ n 3 ​ log ⁡ n n ) = o p ​ ( 1 ) . \|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}\left(\frac{\rho_{n}b_{n}^{9}\log n}{n\tau_{n}^{9}})\right)=o_{p}(1),~~\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}\left(\frac{b_{n}^{3}}{\tau_{n}^{3}}\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}\right)=o_{p}(1). (2) If ρ n ​ b n 9 / τ n 9 = o ⁡ ( n 1 / 2 / ( log ⁡ n ) 1 / 2 ) \rho_{n}b_{n}^{9}/\tau_{n}^{9}=o(n^{1/2}/(\log n)^{1/2}) , then for fixed k k , the vector ( ( β ^ 1 − β 1 ∗ ) , … , ( β ^ k − β k ∗ ) ) ((\widehat{\beta}_{1}-\beta^{*}_{1}),\ldots,(\widehat{\beta}_{k}-\beta^{*}_{k})) follows a k k -dimensional multivariate normal distribution with mean zero and the covariance matrix given by the upper left k × k k\times k block of S S defined at ( 10 ). (3) If b n / τ n = o ⁡ ( n 1 / 24 / ( log ⁡ n ) / 24 ) b_{n}/\tau_{n}=o(n^{1/24}/(\log n)^{/24}) and ρ n ​ b n 9 / τ n 9 = o ⁡ ( ( n / log ⁡ n ) 1 / 2 ) \rho_{n}b_{n}^{9}/\tau_{n}^{9}=o((n/\log n)^{1/2}) , then for arbitrarily given nonzero constant vector c = ( c 1 , … , c p ) ⊤ c=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{p})^{\top} , N ​ c ⊤ ​ ( γ ^ − γ ) \sqrt{N}c^{\top}(\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma) converges in distribution to the normal distribution with mean Σ ¯ − 1 ​ B ∗ \bar{\Sigma}^{-1}B_{*} and variance c ⊤ ​ Σ ¯ ​ c c^{\top}\bar{\Sigma}c . 5 Numerical Studies In this section, we evaluate the asymptotic results of the MLE using simulation studies and a real-world data example. 5.1 Simulation studies We assume that each subject is compared with another subject only once, that is, m i ​ j = 1 m_{ij}=1 for all i ≠ j i\neq j . A comparison between i i and j j is associated with the two-dimensional covariate vector Z i ​ j = ( Z i ​ j ​ 1 , Z i ​ j ​ 2 ) ⊤ Z_{ij}=(Z_{ij1},Z_{ij2})^{\top} . When i &lt; j i&lt;j , Z i ​ j ​ 1 Z_{ij1} takes values − 1 -1 or 1 1 randomly with equal probability, and Z i ​ j ​ 2 Z_{ij2} was generated using a standard normal distribution. Note that Z j ​ i = − Z i ​ j Z_{ji}=-Z_{ij} . All covariates were generated independently. For the parameter 𝜸 ∗ \boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*} , we set γ ∗ = ( 0.5 , 0.5 ) ⊤ \gamma^{*}=(0.5,0.5)^{\top} . We set the merit parameters to be a linear form, i.e., β i ∗ = i ​ c ​ log ⁡ n / n \beta_{i}^{*}=ic\log n/n for i = 0 , … , n i=0,\ldots,n , where max i , j ⁡ ( β i ∗ − β j ∗ ) = c ​ log ⁡ n \max_{i,j}(\beta_{i}^{*}-\beta_{j}^{*})=c\log n . To assess asymptotic properties under different asymptotic regimes, we considered four different values of c c : c = 0 , 0.05 , 0.1 , 0.2 c=0,0.05,0.1,0.2 . From Theorem 3 , ξ ^ i , j = [ β ^ i − β ^ j − ( β i ∗ − β j ∗ ) ] / ( 1 / v ^ i ​ i + 1 / v ^ j ​ j ) 1 / 2 \hat{\xi}_{i,j}=[\hat{\beta}_{i}-\hat{\beta}_{j}-(\beta_{i}^{*}-\beta_{j}^{*})]/(1/\hat{v}_{ii}+1/\hat{v}_{jj})^{1/2} converges in distribution to the standard normality, where v ^ i , i \hat{v}_{i,i} is the estimate of v i , i v_{i,i} by replacing ( 𝜷 ∗ , 𝜸 ∗ ) (\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}) with ( 𝜷 ^ , 𝜸 ^ ) (\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}) . We also recorded the coverage probability of the 95 % 95\% confidence interval and the length of the confidence interval. Each simulation was repeated 5,000 5,000 times. Two values, n = 100 n=100 and n = 200 n=200 , are considered for each participant. The MLE existed in all the simulations. Table 1 reports the coverage probability, the 95 % 95\% confidence interval for ( β i ∗ − β j ∗ ) (\beta_{i}^{*}-\beta_{j}^{*}) and the length of the confidence interval. As we can see, the length of the confidence interval decreases as n n increases, which qualitatively agrees with the theory. Because the difference in the merit parameters between adjacent subjects was very small, the lengths of the confidence intervals were very close across different pairs. This is consistent with the theoretical length. The simulated coverage frequencies are close to the nominal level 95 % 95\% when c = 0 c=0 or c = 0.05 c=0.05 . When c = 0.1 c=0.1 or c = 0.2 c=0.2 , the values are visibly lower than the nominal levels for ( i , j ) = ( 0 , n / 2 ) (i,j)=(0,n/2) and ( 0 , n ) (0,n) . It should be noted that, in these cases, it is more difficult to estimate a large difference between the two merit parameters than for two close merit parameters. This result indicates that by controlling the growth rate of ‖ 𝜷 ∗ ‖ ∞ \|\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\|_{\infty} is necessary to ensure the good properties of the MLE. It contains 30 teams equally divided into Eastern and Western conferences. In the regular season, each team plays two, three, or four games against another, for a total of 82 games, of which 41 games were in their home arena, and 41 were played away. Thus, there were 1, 230 games in the NBA regular season. We used the 2018-19 NBA regular season data as an example, which is available from https://www.landofbasketball.com/yearbyyear/2018_2019_teams.htm . We consider “home/away” as the covariate. When team i i interacts with team j j in the k k th comparison, we set Z i ​ j ​ k = 1 Z_{ijk}=1 if i i is at home; otherwise Z i ​ j ​ k = − 1 Z_{ijk}=-1 . The fitted merits are given in Table 3 , where we use “Washington Wizards” as the baseline ( β n = 0 \beta_{n}=0 ). The estimated home effect γ ^ \widehat{\gamma} and its standard errors are 0.45 0.45 and 0.065 0.065 , respectively. Under the null hypothesis of having no home effects, this gives a p p -value 2.1 × 10 − 12 2.1\times 10^{-12} , indicating a significant home advantage. It would be interesting to compare the order of the eight playoff seeds at the two conferences by the NBA rule, with ordering based on the merits obtained from fitting the Bradley–Terry model. The order from high to low in the eight playoff seeds of the Western conference is as follows: Warriors, Nuggets, Trailblazers, Rockets, Glasses, Thunder, Spurs, and Clippers. The corresponding order at the Eastern Conference was: Bucks, Raptors, 76ers, Celtics, Pacers, Nets, Magics, Pistons. From Table 3 , we see that the ordering of the merits of the Eastern Conference is consistent with that of the NBA rule. In addition, at the Western conference, the order of seven and eight seeds was switched. Table 3: The estimates of β i \beta_{i} and their standard errors in 2018-19 NBA regular season. Using a two-stage process, we demonstrated the consistency of the MLE when the number of subjects approached infinity. Furthermore, by applying a two/third-order Taylor expansion to score functions, we establish the asymptotic normality of the MLE. The strategies for deriving the asymptotic properties of the MLE shed light on a principal approach to similar problems. These principles should apply to a class of paired comparison models, in which the logistic distribution in the Bradley–Terry model is replaced by using other distributions such as the probit distribution in the Thurstone model [ Thurstone 1927 ] even for generalized Bradley–Terry models with ties [ Davidson 1970 ; Rao and Kupper 1967 ]. Note that all results are built on the assumption that all parameters are bounded above by a constant. However, our simulation results indicate that this assumption could be relaxed. Under different conditions imposed on the minimum eigenvalue of the information matrix on the parameter 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} , the consistency and asymptotic normality of the MLE still holds when the range of parameters grows with a slowing rate, as shown in Theorem 5 . Note that the asymptotic behavior of the MLE depends on the configuration of all parameters. It would be interesting to investigate whether these conditions could be relaxed. In addition, we only extend consistency result to a sparse Erdős–Rényi comparison graph with a diverging dimension of covariates. When the dimension of covariates, p n p_{n} , increases, the convergence rate the MLE of its regression coefficient becomes slow as shown in Theorem 4 . Different diverging rates of p n p_{n} have different influences on asymptotic distribution. We would like to investigate this problem in future studies. 7 Appendix 7.1 Preliminaries In this section, we present some preliminary results, which shall be used in the proofs. The first is on the approximation error of using S S in ( 10 ) to approximate the inverse of V V belonging to the matrix class ℒ n ​ ( b 0 , b 1 ) \mathcal{L}_{n}(b_{0},b_{1}) . Simons and Yao 1998 obtained the upper bound of the approximation error ‖ V − 1 − S ‖ max ≤ 1 n 2 ​ ( 1 + b 1 b 0 ) ​ b 1 2 b 0 3 = O ⁡ ( b 1 2 n 2 ​ b 0 3 ) , \|V^{-1}-S\|_{\max}\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\left(1+\frac{b_{1}}{b_{0}}\right)\frac{b_{1}^{2}}{b_{0}^{3}}=O\left(\frac{b_{1}^{2}}{n^{2}b_{0}^{3}}\right), (16) where V ∈ ℒ n ​ ( b 0 , b 1 ) V\in\mathcal{L}_{n}(b_{0},b_{1}) for two positive numbers b 0 b_{0} and b 1 b_{1} with b 0 ≤ b 1 b_{0}\leq b_{1} , and ‖ A ‖ max = max i , j ⁡ | a i ​ j | \|A\|_{\max}=\max_{i,j}|a_{ij}| for a general matrix A A . Next, we present some useful inequalities that will be repeatedly used in the proof. Recall that μ ⁡ ( x ) = e x / ( 1 + e x ) \mu(x)=e^{x}/(1+e^{x}) . Write μ ′ \mu^{\prime} , μ ′′ \mu^{\prime\prime} and μ ′′′ \mu^{\prime\prime\prime} as the first, second and third derivative of μ ⁡ ( x ) \mu(x) on x x , respectively. Therefore, we have [ W ⁡ ( 𝐝 − 𝔼 ​ 𝐝 ) ] i = O p ​ ( log ⁡ n n ) . [W(\mathbf{d}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{d})]_{i}=O_{p}(\frac{\log n}{n}). By ( 32 ), we have β ^ i − β i ∗ = [ S ( 𝐝 − 𝔼 𝐝 ) ] i + o p ( n − 1 / 2 ) . \widehat{\beta}_{i}-\beta^{*}_{i}=[S(\mathbf{d}-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{d})]_{i}+o_{p}(n^{-1/2}). Therefore, Theorem 3 immediately comes from Proposition 2 . ∎ References Agresti (2012) Agresti, A. (2012). Categorical Data Analysis, 3rd Edition . Wiley, New York. There are numerous convergence results on the Newton method. We use the result in Yamamoto 1986 , whose conditions are relatively easy to verify in our case. Lemma 6 ( Yamamoto 1986 ) . Let X X and Y Y be Banach spaces, D D be an open convex subset of X X and F : D ⊆ X → Y F:D\subseteq X\to Y be Fréchet differentiable. Assume that, at some 𝐱 0 ∈ D \mathbf{x}_{0}\in D , F ′ ​ ( 𝐱 0 ) F^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}_{0}) is invertible and that ‖ F ′ ​ ( 𝐱 0 ) − 1 ​ ( F ′ ​ ( 𝐱 ) − F ′ ​ ( 𝐲 ) ) ‖ ≤ K ​ ‖ 𝐱 − 𝐲 ‖ , 𝐱 , 𝐲 ∈ D , \displaystyle\|F^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}_{0})^{-1}(F^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})-F^{\prime}(\mathbf{y}))\|\leq K\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|,~~\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in D, (37) ‖ F ′ ​ ( 𝐱 0 ) − 1 ​ F ​ ( 𝐱 0 ) ‖ ≤ η , h = K ​ η ≤ 1 / 2 , \displaystyle\|F^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}_{0})^{-1}F(\mathbf{x}_{0})\|\leq\eta,~~h=K\eta\leq 1/2, (38) S ¯ ​ ( 𝐱 0 , t ∗ ) ⊆ D , t ∗ = 2 ​ η / ( 1 + 1 − 2 ​ h ) , \displaystyle\bar{S}(\mathbf{x}_{0},t^{*})\subseteq D,~~t^{*}=2\eta/(1+\sqrt{1-2h}), where ∥ ⋅ ∥ \|\cdot\| denotes a general norm on vectors. Then: (1) The Newton iterates 𝐱 n + 1 = 𝐱 n − F ′ ​ ( 𝐱 n ) − 1 ​ F ​ ( 𝐱 n ) \mathbf{x}_{n+1}=\mathbf{x}_{n}-F^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}_{n})^{-1}F(\mathbf{x}_{n}) , n ≥ 0 n\geq 0 are well-defined, lie in S ¯ ​ ( 𝐱 0 , t ∗ ) \bar{S}(\mathbf{x}_{0},t^{*}) and converge to a solution 𝐱 ∗ \mathbf{x}^{*} of F ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) = 0 F(\mathbf{x})=0 . (2) The solution 𝐱 ∗ \mathbf{x}^{*} is unique in S ⁡ ( 𝐱 0 , t ∗ ⁣ ∗ ) ∩ D S(\mathbf{x}_{0},t^{**})\cap D , t ∗ ⁣ ∗ = ( 1 + 1 − 2 ​ h ) / K t^{**}=(1+\sqrt{1-2h})/K if 2 ​ h &lt; 1 2h&lt;1 and in S ¯ ​ ( 𝐱 0 , t ∗ ⁣ ∗ ) \bar{S}(\mathbf{x}_{0},t^{**}) if 2 ​ h = 1 2h=1 . (3) ‖ 𝐱 ∗ − 𝐱 n ‖ ≤ t ∗ \|\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{x}_{n}\|\leq t^{*} if n = 0 n=0 and ‖ 𝐱 ∗ − 𝐱 n ‖ ≤ 2 1 − n ​ ( 2 ​ h ) 2 n − 1 ​ η \|\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{x}_{n}\|\leq 2^{1-n}(2h)^{2^{n}-1}\eta if n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 . Before proving Lemma 2 , we show one lemma. The following lemma shows that the Jacobian matrix H γ ′ ​ ( 𝜷 ) H^{\prime}_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\beta}) of H γ ​ ( 𝜷 ) H_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\beta}) is Lipschitz continuous. Lemma 7 . Let D = B ⁡ ( 𝛃 ∗ , ϵ n ​ 1 ) ( ⊂ ℝ n ) D=B(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*},\epsilon_{n1})(\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}) be an open convex set containing the true point 𝛃 ∗ \boldsymbol{\beta}^{*} . For any 𝛄 ∈ ℝ \boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R} , the following holds: max i = 0 , … , n ⁡ ‖ H γ , i ′ ​ ( 𝐱 ) − H γ , i ′ ​ ( 𝐲 ) ‖ 1 ≤ max i = 0 , … , n ⁡ m i . \max_{i=0,\ldots,n}\|H^{\prime}_{\gamma,i}(\mathbf{x})-H^{\prime}_{\gamma,i}(\mathbf{y})\|_{1}\leq\max_{i=0,\ldots,n}m_{i}. Proof of Lemma 7 . Recall that H i ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) = ∑ j ≠ i ∑ k μ ( β i − β j + Z i ​ j ​ k ⊤ 𝜸 ) − d i , i = 0 , … , n . H_{i}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{k}\mu(\beta_{i}-\beta_{j}+Z_{ijk}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma})-d_{i},~~i=0,\ldots,n. and H γ , i ​ ( 𝜷 ) H_{\gamma,i}(\boldsymbol{\beta}) is the version of H i ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) H_{i}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) by treating 𝜸 \boldsymbol{\gamma} as a fixed parameter. The Jacobian matrix H γ , i ′ ​ ( 𝜷 ) H^{\prime}_{\gamma,i}(\boldsymbol{\beta}) of H γ , i ​ ( 𝜷 ) H_{\gamma,i}(\boldsymbol{\beta}) can be calculated as follows. For a nonlinear equation, Ortega and Rheinboldt 1970 gives a simple sufficient condition to guarantee the existence of the solution, stated below. Lemma 8 (Theorem 6.3.4 in Ortega and Rheinboldt 1970 ) . Let C C be an open, bounded set in ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} , C ˙ \dot{C} be the boundary of the set C C and S ¯ \bar{S} be the closure of the set C C . Assume that F : C ¯ ⊂ ℝ n → ℝ n F:\bar{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n} is continuous and satisfies ( x − x 0 ) ⊤ ​ F ​ ( x ) ≥ 0 (x-x^{0})^{\top}F(x)\geq 0 for some x 0 ∈ C x^{0}\in C and all x ∈ C 0 x\in C^{0} . Then F ⁡ ( x ) = 0 F(x)=0 has a solution in C ¯ \bar{C} . To show ( 𝜸 − 𝜸 ∗ ) ⊤ ​ Q β ​ ( 𝜸 ) ≥ 0 (\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*})^{\top}Q_{\beta}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})\geq 0 , in view of Lemma 8 , it is sufficient to verify the following condition: there exists a constant Δ &gt; 0 \Delta&gt;0 such that for all sufficiently large n n , sup ‖ 𝜸 − 𝜸 0 ‖ 2 = Δ ​ p n ​ log ⁡ n n ( 𝜸 − 𝜸 0 ) ⊤ ​ Q β ​ ( 𝜸 ) &gt; 0 . \sup_{\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{0}\|_{2}=\Delta\sqrt{\frac{p_{n}\log n}{n}}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{0})^{\top}Q_{\beta}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})&gt;0. (43) Portnoy 1984 applied this technique to establish the existence and consistency of M M -estimator for independently identically distributed data. In a different setting, Wang 2011b used it to analyze generalized estimating equations (GEE) of clustered binary data. We prove a general version of Lemma 3 , which will be used to show consistency in case of a diverging number of covariates. Lemma 9 . B.4 Proof of ( 26 ): Bound of S 3 S_{3} In this section we show ( 26 ). We calculate g k ​ l ​ h i ​ j = ∂ 3 μ i ​ j ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) ∂ β k ​ ∂ β l ​ ∂ β h g^{ij}_{klh}=\frac{\partial^{3}\mu_{ij}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma})}{\partial\beta_{k}\partial\beta_{l}\partial\beta_{h}} according to the indices k , l , h k,l,h as follows. We first observe that g k ​ l ​ h i ​ j = 0 g^{ij}_{klh}=0 when k , l , h ∉ { i , j } k,l,h\notin\{i,j\} since μ i ​ j ​ ( 𝜷 , 𝜸 ) \mu_{ij}(\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}) only has the arguments β i \beta_{i} and β j \beta_{j} in regardless of other β k \beta_{k} ’s ( k ≠ i , j k\neq i,j ). So there are only two cases below in which g k ​ l ​ h i ​ j ≠ 0 g^{ij}_{klh}\neq 0 . (1) Only two values among three indices k , l , h k,l,h are equal. Finally, applying Lemma 11 , we obtain the desired bound for the third conclusion. It completes the proof. ∎ Lemma 16 .
